Surface polarization profile of ferroelectric thin films probed by X-ray standing waves and photoelectron spectroscopy
Authors: Le Phuong Hoang, Irena Spasojevic, Tien-Lin Lee, David Pesquera, Kai Rossnagel, Jörg Zegenhagen, Gustau Catalan, Ivan A. Vartanyants, Andreas Scherz, Giuseppe Mercurio
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
Understanding the mechanisms underlying a stable polarization at the surface of ferroelectric thin films is of particular importance both from a fundamental point of view and to achieve control of the surface polarization itself. In this study, we demonstrate that the X-ray standing wave technique allows the surface polarization profile of a ferroelectric thin film, as opposed to the average film polarity, to be probed directly. The X-ray standing wave technique provides the average Ti and Ba atomic positions, along the out-of-plane direction, near the surface of three differently strained \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {BaTiO_3}$$\end{document} thin films. This technique gives direct access to the local ferroelectric polarization at and below the surface. By employing X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy, a detailed overview of the oxygen-containing species adsorbed on the surface is obtained. The different amplitude and orientation of the local ferroelectric polarizations are associated with surface charges attributed to different type, amount and spatial distribution of the oxygen-containing adsorbates.

Introduction

Ferroelectric thin films have attracted great scientific interest due to their properties, such as switchable polarization, ferroelasticity, piezoelectricity, and pyroelectricity, which are crucial for technological applications1–4. Displacive ferroelectrics, such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {BaTiO}_3$$\end{document} (BTO), exhibit an intrinsic spontaneous polarization associated with the relative displacement of cations and anions within the unit cell5. This polarization can be manipulated by varying the lattice parameters of the ferroelectrics6,7. To this end, ferroelectric thin films have been grown on substrates with different lattice constants. The lattice mismatch can induce uniform strain or strain gradients in the thin films8. Suitable substrates and bottom electrodes have been employed to tune the ferroelectric polarization, which is typically measured by piezoresponse force microscopy (PFM), a technique that is sensitive to the average polarization of the entire film, for thicknesses of a few tens of nanometers9,10. The distribution of the ferroelectric polarization at the surface, which can differ from that of the bulk, has been investigated theoretically as a function of various parameters, such as surface termination and adsorbates11,12. However, an experimental method that can simultaneously probe the surface ferroelectric polarization and the chemical composition of the adsorbates is still lacking.

The determination of the surface polarization has a twofold relevance. First, from a fundamental point of view, uncompensated charges at the surface of a ferroelectric thin film can be screened, among several mechanisms, by external charges provided by adsorbates13–17 or can lead to a reconstruction of the top unit cells to minimize the surface energy18–20. This, in turn, can influence the polarization of deeper layers, and, for very thin films, affect the polarization of the entire sample21. Therefore, in order to control the ferroelectric polarization of a thin film, probing it and understanding its stabilization mechanisms near the surface are of particular importance. Second, from the point of view of promising applications, ferroelectrics have been proposed as catalysts with chemical activity that is switchable between reducing and oxidizing surfaces depending on the polarization direction22–27. In this context, determining the surface polarization is the first step towards the development of efficient ferroelectric catalysts.

Among non-destructive techniques employed to determine atomic positions at (and near) the surface, and thus the microscopic origin of ferroelectric polarization, crystal truncation rod (CTR) scattering and low-energy electron diffraction (LEED-IV) have been successfully employed to reveal atomic structures with approximately \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm 10\hbox { pm}$$\end{document} accuracy13–15,18,28. However, neither of the above methods provides spectroscopic information on atoms in ferroelectric thin films and adsorbate species. This could be achieved by X-ray photoelectron diffraction, however, at the expense of rather complex multiple-scattering simulations29–33.

In this work, we employ the X-ray standing wave (XSW) technique, a combination of X-ray diffraction (XRD) and X-ray spectroscopy, to determine atomic positions with picometer accuracy and chemical specificity. The structural accuracy of this technique for determining atomic positions in single crystals and adsorbates on crystal surfaces has been demonstrated extensively34–38. Furthermore, the XSW technique proved to be successful in determining the average polarity of non-centrosymmetric single crystals39 and thin films40,41, as well as ferroelectric thin films42,43. In the latter experiments, the average polarity of the thin films was determined by combining XSW and X-ray fluorescence spectroscopy (XFS). Here, we apply the XSW technique, in combination with X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy (XPS), a more surface sensitive technique than XFS, to measure the displacement of Ti atoms from the center of the unit cell in differently strained BTO thin films, and thereby deduce the ferroelectric polarization at different depths near the surface. These data are interpreted in the context of the average film polarization measured by PFM, as well as the type, content and spatial distribution of adsorbates on the sample surface. This combination of the structural sensitivity of XSW with the chemical specificity and depth selectivity of XPS, provides a detailed insight into the near-surface polarization profile and its interplay with adsorbates and the bulk ferroelectric polarization.

X-ray standing waves generated in thin films

The XSW technique is particularly useful for determining atomic positions in crystals, surfaces, and their adsorbates34–38. The interference between incoming and Bragg-diffracted X-ray plane waves in a perfect crystal results in an X-ray standing wave field with the following sinusoidal modulation of the X-ray intensity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_\textrm{XSW}$$\end{document} (Fig. 1a,b):1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I_\textrm{XSW}\left( E_\nu \right) \propto 1 + \left| \frac{\mathscr {E}_\textrm{H}}{\mathscr {E}_\textrm{0}}\right| ^2 + 2 \left| \frac{\mathscr {E}_\textrm{H}}{\mathscr {E}_\textrm{0}}\right| \cos \left( \alpha (E_\nu ) + \varvec{h}\varvec{r} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{h} = 2 \pi \varvec{H}$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{H}$$\end{document} is the reciprocal lattice vector. In Eq. (1), the three terms represent the incident, Bragg-diffracted, and interference X-ray wave, respectively. As the incident photon energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_\nu$$\end{document} varies through the (hkl) Bragg reflection, the phase \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha (E_\nu )$$\end{document} between the Bragg-diffracted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {E}_\textrm{H}$$\end{document} and incident \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {E}_0$$\end{document} electric field amplitudes changes by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi$$\end{document}. This leads to a shift of the XSW field along \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{H}$$\end{document} by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_\textrm{hkl}/2$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_\textrm{hkl} = |\varvec{H} |^{-1}$$\end{document} is the spacing between two consecutive (hkl) atomic planes (Fig. 1b). Atoms at different positions in the unit cell experience different X-ray absorption and hence give rise to different photoelectron (PE) yield as a function of the photon energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_\nu$$\end{document}. In a typical ferroelectric thin film grown on a substrate, the lattice mismatch may lead to strain gradients in the epitaxial layers8. Therefore, thin films are generally characterized by a deformation field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{u}\left( z\right)$$\end{document}, which defines the actual displacement of atoms from the corresponding position in a perfect crystal, and the static Debye–Waller factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{e}^{-W(z)}$$\end{document}, which accounts for random displacement of atoms from their average position along the z direction (Fig. 1a). In contrast to the perfect crystal case above, the XSW generated in a thin film is modified by the deformation phase \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \left( z\right) = \varvec{h} \cdot \varvec{u}\left( z\right)$$\end{document} due to the crystal deformation field. Based on the dynamical theory of diffraction, the Takagi–Taupin equations44–46 describe the propagation of X-rays in a deformed crystal, and thus give the following XSW intensity generated in a typical ferroelectric thin film:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I_\textrm{XSW}\left( E_\nu ,z\right) = 1 + R\left( E_\nu ,z\right) + 2C \sqrt{R\left( E_\nu ,z\right) } \textrm{e}^{-W\left( z\right) }\cos \left( \alpha (E_\nu ,z) + \varphi \left( z\right) + 2\pi z/d_\textrm{hkl} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0(E_\nu ) = R(E_\nu ,0)$$\end{document} indicates the observable X-ray diffracted intensity at the sample surface (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z=0$$\end{document}), and the parameter C depends on the X-ray polarization47 (see “Methods”, “Reflection and transmission calculation”). Based on the XSW generated in a ferroelectric thin film of thickness t, from the PE yield curve3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \kappa ^s_\gamma (E_\nu ) = I_0^{-1} \int _{0}^{t_{L_0}} dz \rho _\textrm{yi}\left( E_\nu ,z,\gamma \right) |T\left( E_\nu ,z\right) |^2 \Big [1 + R\left( E_\nu ,z\right) + 2C \sqrt{R\left( E_\nu ,z\right) } F^s_{c,\gamma } \cos \left( \alpha (E_\nu ,z) + \varphi _0 + 2 \pi P^s_{c,\gamma } \right) \Big ], \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}we can determine the average position and distribution of atoms s, which are defined as the coherent position \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P^s_{c,\gamma }$$\end{document} and coherent fraction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^s_{c,\gamma }$$\end{document}, respectively. These parameters are equivalent to the phase and amplitude of the structure factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{S}^s_{\varvec{h}} = \sum _{j} \exp (\textrm{i}\varvec{h} \varvec{r}_j^s ) = \left| \varvec{S}^s_{\varvec{h}}\right| \exp (\textrm{i} \varphi ^s_{\varvec{h},\gamma })$$\end{document} referring to the positions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{r}_j^s$$\end{document} of atoms s in the unit cell. More specifically, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P^s_{c,\gamma } = \varphi ^s_{\varvec{h},\gamma } / (2 \pi )$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^s_{c,\gamma } = |\varvec{S}^s_{\varvec{h}}| {\textrm{e}^{-W_0} \textrm{e}^{-W^\textrm{T}}}$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{e}^{-W_0}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{e}^{-W^\textrm{T}}$$\end{document} are the Debye–Waller factors accounting for static and thermal atomic displacements. Therefore, the absolute average position of atoms s within the unit cell along \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{H}$$\end{document} is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z^s_\gamma = P^s_{c,\gamma } d_\textrm{hkl}$$\end{document}, and their spatial distribution is characterized by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^s_{c,\gamma }$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<P^s_{c,\gamma }<1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<F^s_{c,\gamma }<1$$\end{document}. In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^s_{c,\gamma } = 1$$\end{document} refers to all atoms at the same z, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^s_{c,\gamma } = 0$$\end{document} corresponds to a uniform distribution of two or more atomic positions across the unit cell.

In Eq. (3), the PE yield function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa ^s_\gamma (E_\nu )$$\end{document} is the sum of yield contributions from atoms in the top layer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_0$$\end{document} at positions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<z<t_{L_0}$$\end{document}, weighted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _\textrm{yi}(E_\nu ,z,\gamma )$$\end{document}, with XSW transmission \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\left( E_\nu ,z\right)$$\end{document}, and normalization factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_0 = \int _{0}^{t_{L_0}} dz \rho _\textrm{yi}(E_\nu ,z,\gamma )$$\end{document}. The function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _\textrm{yi}(E_\nu ,z,\gamma ) = \exp \left( -z/\lambda _{l,\gamma }\right)$$\end{document} gives the probability of detecting a photoelectron from the atomic core level l at depth z, with exit angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document} from the sample surface (Fig. 1c). The parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{l,\gamma } = \lambda _{l}(E_\nu ) \sin \gamma$$\end{document} is the electron escape depth48, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _{l}\left( E_\nu \right)$$\end{document} indicates the inelastic mean free path (IMFP), or more correctly the effective attenuation length (EAL) that includes elastic scattering effects49. Tuning \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document} allows the surface sensitivity to be varied and provides average atomic positions over different depths from the surface. To determine the average distribution of atoms s at a given exit angle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document}, the measured PE yield curve is fitted with Eq. (3) using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P^s_{c,\gamma }$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^s_{c,\gamma }$$\end{document} as fit parameters. In fact, all other quantities in Eq. (3) can be calculated from known sample properties (see “Methods”, “Reflection and transmission calculation”), or derived from the fit of Bragg reflectivity data, e.g., the average amorphization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{e}^{-W_0}$$\end{document} of layer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_0$$\end{document} and the corresponding deformation phase \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _0$$\end{document} (see “Results” and “Methods”, “Deformation phase calculation”).

Results

Sample characterization

The samples investigated in this work are BTO ferroelectric thin films grown on three different substrates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {DyScO}_3$$\end{document} (DSO), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {GdScO}_3$$\end{document} (GSO), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {SmScO}_3$$\end{document} (SSO), with a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {SrRuO}_3$$\end{document} (SRO) thin film in between serving as the bottom electrode. Sample growth was performed by means of pulsed laser deposition (see “Methods”, “Sample growth”) on substrates with (001) orientation according to the pseudocubic notation50. At the given growth conditions, BTO thin films have mixed (BaO and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {TiO}_2$$\end{document}) termination. The thickness of the BTO and SRO thin films was determined by grazing X-ray reflectivity (see “Methods”, “Grazing X-ray reflectivity” and Supplementary Fig. 1). The SRO layers have thicknesses in the range of 20–26 nm, while the BTO layers are 20 nm, 37 nm, and 35.5 nm thick on DSO, GSO, and SSO, respectively (Table 1). To determine the average in-plane strain of the thin films, X-ray reciprocal space maps (RSM) around the (-103) substrate Bragg peak were measured (see “Methods”, “X-ray reciprocal lattice map”). Results reported in Fig. 2a–c show that all BTO and SRO thin films are coherently strained to their underlying substrates, without any relaxation of the in-plane lattice parameter. The in-plane strain applied by a substrate to the BTO thin film is calculated as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^{a}_\textrm{BTO} = \left( a_\textrm{BTO} - a_\textrm{b,BTO} \right) / a_\textrm{b,BTO}$$\end{document}, by comparing the measured in-plane lattice parameter of the thin film \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_\textrm{BTO}$$\end{document} (see “Methods”, “X-ray reciprocal lattice map”) with the respective bulk value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{b,\textrm{BTO}} = 3.992$$\end{document} Å5. As a result, the in-plane compressive strain in the BTO thin film is smallest on the SSO substrate (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-0.38\%$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{b,\textrm{SSO}} =$$\end{document} 3.977 Å51), it increases on GSO (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-0.63\%$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{b,\textrm{GSO}} =$$\end{document} 3.967 Å51), and is largest on DSO (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$-1.23\%$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_{b,\textrm{DSO}} =$$\end{document} 3.943 Å51) (Table 1). The in-plane compressive strain in BTO thin films induces an out-of-plane spontaneous ferroelectric polarization6. To determine the average polarization orientation of the as-grown BTO thin films, and to verify that all samples can be electrically switched, piezoresponse force microscopy (PFM) was employed. PFM data (Fig. 2d–f and “Methods”, “Piezoresponse force microscopy”) show that the average BTO polarization of BTO/SRO/DSO is down (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{P}^\downarrow$$\end{document}), while in the other two samples it is up (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{P}^\uparrow$$\end{document}), i.e., with the Ti atom below (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{P}^\downarrow$$\end{document}) or above (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{P}^\uparrow$$\end{document}) the center of the oxygen octahedra (Fig. 1b). The different average polarization direction of the three BTO samples results from the complex interplay of the electronic structure and the chemistry of the surface and the interface to the bottom layer11,52,53. Furthermore, both the absence of side peaks in RSM data and the presence of a homogeneous as-grown phase measured by PFM support the presence of a single domain in our samples. This ensures that atomic positions of the same structural phase are measured by XSW measurements. Finally, the analysis of AFM images provides a root mean square surface roughness \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_\textrm{RMS}$$\end{document} of approximately 0.84(8) nm (Table 1), in line with Ref.54.Table 1BTO and SRO layer thicknesses, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_\textrm{BTO}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_\textrm{SRO}$$\end{document}, resulting from grazing X-ray reflectivity (see “Methods”, “Grazing X-ray reflectivity” and Supplementary Fig. 1). All substrates are 0.5 mm thick. The root mean square surface roughness \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_\textrm{RMS}$$\end{document} of BTO thin films was determined from AFM images of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3.9\times 3.9\,\upmu \hbox {m}^2$$\end{document} area. All substrates are 0.5 mm thick. The root mean square surface roughness \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_\textrm{RMS}$$\end{document} of BTO thin films was determined from AFM images of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$3.9\times 3.9\,\upmu \hbox {m}^2$$\end{document} area. Compressive in-plane strain in BTO (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^{a}_\textrm{BTO}$$\end{document}) thin films, measured by X-ray reciprocal space maps (Fig. 2a–c). Orientation of the average BTO polarization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{P}$$\end{document} measured by PFM (Fig. 2d–f).

Reciprocal space maps around (-103) substrate Bragg peak of the BTO/SRO/DSO (a), BTO/SRO/GSO (b) and BTO/SRO/SSO (c) samples. The gray vertical dashed lines indicate the reciprocal lattice parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\textrm{x}$$\end{document} shared by the substrate, the BTO, and SRO thin films in each sample. PFM phase images of the BTO/SRO/DSO (d), BTO/SRO/GSO (e), and BTO/SRO/SSO (f) samples. The sign of the applied tip voltage (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm \textrm{V}$$\end{document}) within the gray boxes and the resulting average polarization direction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{P}^\uparrow$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\odot$$\end{document}) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{P}^\downarrow$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\otimes$$\end{document}) in the probed areas are marked on each panel. The PFM phase beyond the gray boxes indicates the average polarization direction of as-grown samples.

X-ray standing wave setup

XSW experiments were performed at the I09 beamline of Diamond Light Source55. The soft X-ray branch of I09, equipped with a plane grating monochromator, delivered an X-ray beam of approximately \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$300\,\upmu \hbox {m} \times 200\,\upmu \hbox {m}$$\end{document} full width at half maximum (FWHM) at the sample. Each XSW measurement consisted of recording Bragg reflections and simultaneously photoelectron spectra of the sample. The Bragg reflections were measured by scanning the photon energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_\nu$$\end{document} with the incoming X-ray beam impinging on the sample at a fixed angle of incidence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\theta } = 87^{\circ }$$\end{document} (Fig. 1c). The intensity of the diffracted X-ray beam was measured by a Si photodiode with a central through hole for the incident beam to pass (Fig. 1c). With this experimental geometry, the (001) Bragg reflections of the BTO and SRO films and the substrates were recorded within the range of photon energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_\nu$$\end{document} from 1400 eV to 1700 eV. Simultaneously, XPS spectra were measured by a Scienta EW4000 electron analyzer, with the detection system consisting of a microchannel plate (MCP) followed by a charge-coupled device (CCD). The wide acceptance angle of the electron analyzer enabled parallel measurements of spectra over three different exit angle ranges: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _1$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$7.8^{\circ } \pm 5.4^{\circ }$$\end{document}), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _2$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$18.5^{\circ } \pm 5.4^{\circ }$$\end{document}) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _3$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$27.4^{\circ } \pm 3.6^{\circ }$$\end{document}). This provides the chemical and structural information of the BTO films with increasing depth sensitivity from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _1$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _3$$\end{document} (Fig. 1c). The overall spectral energy resolution, limited by the X-ray bandwidth, was approximately 400 meV. The (001) Bragg reflections, XPS and XSW experimental results are reported in the following sections.

(001) Bragg reflections

To determine the atomic positions at the BTO surface along the out-of-plane polarization direction, the (001) Bragg reflection was chosen for the XSW experiments. Figure 3a shows the reflectivity curves around the (001) reflections of the BTO, SRO, and substrates. The reflections of the substrates are more than two orders of magnitude stronger and much narrower compared to those of the thin layers. The low intensity and the broadening of the thin film Bragg peaks result from two factors: the finite film thickness and the inhomogeneous strain. More specifically, the reflectivity of BTO [SRO] films lies in the range of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.03\% {-} 0.05\%$$\end{document} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left[ 0.01\% {-} 0.02\% \right],$$\end{document} whereas the substrate reflectivity is of the order of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$10\%$$\end{document} for all samples. Going from SSO, to GSO, and then to DSO, the substrate Bragg peak gradually shifts to higher photon energies, as expected from the decreasing trend of the bulk out-of-plane lattice parameters of the substrates (Fig. 2a–c). This trend suggests that the in-plane strain in the films becomes more compressive in BTO and less tensile in SRO (see Supplementary Note 1) in the same substrate order. As a result, the average c parameters of BTO (see “Methods”, “Average out-of-plane lattice parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{c}$$\end{document}”) increase from the SSO to the DSO sample: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{c}_\textrm{BTO} =$$\end{document} 4.055 (22) Å, 4.063(23) Å, 4.070(45) Å, respectively. These values are in quantitative agreement with Ref.6 and with calculations, assuming a Poisson’s ratio of 0.3156. For the BTO thin films, the measured \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{c}_\textrm{BTO}$$\end{document} parameters correspond to an average out-of-plane strain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\varepsilon }^c_\textrm{BTO} = \left( \overline{c}_\textrm{BTO} - c_{b,\textrm{BTO}} \right) / c_{b,\textrm{BTO}}$$\end{document} of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.48\%$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.68\%$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0.84\%$$\end{document} for SSO, GSO and DSO, respectively, with the bulk out-of-plane lattice parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_{b,\textrm{BTO}} =$$\end{document} 4.036 Å5.Figure 3(a) (001) Bragg reflectivity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0(E_\nu )$$\end{document} (points) and corresponding fit curves (solid lines) of samples BTO/SRO/DSO (pink), BTO/SRO/GSO (cyan) and BTO/SRO/SSO (green). (b) BTO out-of-plane lattice parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_i$$\end{document} (solid lines) in sublayers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_i$$\end{document} and c(z) (dotted lines) based on Eq. (4) and a linear distribution of c (see Section (001) Bragg reflections). The average BTO out-of-plane lattice parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{c}_\textrm{BTO}$$\end{document} are marked by black stars.

(a) (001) Bragg reflectivity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0(E_\nu )$$\end{document} (points) and corresponding fit curves (solid lines) of samples BTO/SRO/DSO (pink), BTO/SRO/GSO (cyan) and BTO/SRO/SSO (green). (b) BTO out-of-plane lattice parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_i$$\end{document} (solid lines) in sublayers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_i$$\end{document} and c(z) (dotted lines) based on Eq. (4) and a linear distribution of c (see Section (001) Bragg reflections). The average BTO out-of-plane lattice parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{c}_\textrm{BTO}$$\end{document} are marked by black stars.

As anticipated above, an epitaxial thin film may be characterized by an inhomogeneous out-of-plane strain. According to the general strain profile model discussed in Refs.8,57, the strain gradient is expected to be proportional to the strain, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\partial \varepsilon }^c/{\partial z} \propto \varepsilon ^c$$\end{document}, independently of the actual relaxation mechanism. As a result, the out-of-plane parameter c follows an exponential dependence on z:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} c(z) = c_b \left( 1 + \varepsilon _\textrm{int}^c \textrm{e}^{-(t-z)/\delta } \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _\textrm{int}^c = (c_\textrm{int} -c_b)/c_b$$\end{document} is the strain at the interface with the underlying layer (or substrate), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document} is the penetration depth of strain that is inversely proportional to the strain gradient. This model has been successfully applied to ferroelectric thin films of several 100s nm8,57. In a recent work58,59, it was also found that 50 nm thick \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {PbTiO_3}$$\end{document} films, displaying high crystalline quality and no indication of in-plane relaxation, could be well described by an exponential profile of c parameters, as further confirmed by TEM images. In this case59, the strain gradient was assigned to a compositional gradient of lead oxide dipolar vacancies. A similar distribution of vacancies or defects could be present also in our samples and may underlay the presence of strain gradients. In fact, modelling our BTO and SRO thin films with a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{c}$$\end{document} leads to unsatisfactory fit results. To fit the experimental reflectivity curves in Fig. 3, BTO and SRO layers are divided into n sublayers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_i$$\end{document} (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i = 0, \ldots , n-1$$\end{document}) of equal thickness \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_i$$\end{document} with an out-of-plane lattice parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_i$$\end{document} varying exponentially with i as described by Eq. (4), Debye–Waller factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{e}^{-W_i}$$\end{document}, and deformation phase \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi _i = 2 \pi ( c_i - \overline{c})t_i/\overline{c}^2$$\end{document} (see “Methods”, “Deformation phase calculation”). In our samples, the minimum common number of sublayers necessary to accurately describe them is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=5$$\end{document}. Increasing the number of sublayers n does not improve the fit. For the BTO thin film of the BTO/SRO/DSO sample, both the exponential c distribution of Eq. (4) and the linear c distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {c(z)} = c_b \left( 1 + \varepsilon _\textrm{int}^c -\delta _\textrm{lin}(t-z) \right)$$\end{document} were employed and compared in Supplementary Fig. 3. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _\textrm{lin}$$\end{document} is the rate of strain change with z. We observe that a linear c distribution can reproduce the experimental data better than the exponential one for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$20\hbox { nm}$$\end{document} BTO layer in BTO/SRO/DSO. A similar observation based on TEM studies was reported for films with thickness \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\le 20\hbox { nm}$$\end{document}60,61. We note also that no improvement in the fit was observed when applying the linear distribution to the thicker BTO layers or to the bottom SRO layers. Experimental data in Fig. 3a are fitted with the reflectivity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_0(E_\nu )$$\end{document} using the fitting parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{e}^{-W_i}$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=0, \ldots ,4$$\end{document}), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _\textrm{int}^c$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document} (or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _\textrm{lin}$$\end{document}) for the BTO and SRO layer (see Section X-ray standing waves generated in thin films and “Methods”, “Reflection and transmission calculation”).

The best fits to the reflectivity curves shown in Fig. 3a reproduce reasonably well the main features of the experimental data, with deviations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$< 5 \times 10^{-3}\%$$\end{document}. This validates the models used to capture the main structural features of our samples. However, the small differences between experimental data and fit curves suggest a more complex strain distribution which cannot be mimicked by the relatively simple strain models presented above. The resulting out-of-plane parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_i$$\end{document} are shown in Fig. 3b and in Supplementary Fig. 4. The Debye–Waller factors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{e}^{-W_i}$$\end{document} in BTO sublayers are mostly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ge 0.9$$\end{document} with lower values at the interface to SRO (Supplementary Table 1). A similar observation of larger structural disorder at the interface to the layer below has been revealed by transmission electron microscopy (TEM) studies60–62. The fit parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _\textrm{int}^c$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta$$\end{document} of BTO, in the BTO/SRO/GSO and BTO/SRO/SSO samples, show a direct and inverse proportionality to the in-plane compressive strain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^{a}_\textrm{BTO}$$\end{document}, respectively (Supplementary Table 2). In fact, a larger in-plane compressive strain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon ^a_\textrm{BTO}$$\end{document} leads to larger average out-of-plane strain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\varepsilon }^c_\textrm{BTO}$$\end{document} and therefore, a larger strain gradient. Similar and even larger strain gradients, as compared to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\partial \varepsilon ^c}/{\partial z} =$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1.5\times 10^{-4}\hbox { nm}^{-1}$$\end{document} in BTO/SRO/DSO, have been previously observed by TEM and CTR scattering measurements on BTO thin films15,60 and other ferroelectrics59,61,62. Interestingly, despite the different in-plane compressive strains in the three samples (Table 1), the strain gradients lead to similar out-of-plane lattice parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_0$$\end{document} at the top sublayer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_0$$\end{document}: 4.02 Å, 4.046 Å, 4.045 Å. Subsequently, the fitting of the experimental reflectivity curves in Fig. 3a provides the necessary structural data to calculate the PE yield fit function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa ^s_\gamma (E_\nu )$$\end{document} in Eq. (3). We turn now to the determination of the experimental PE yield from XPS spectra.

Ba, Ti and O XPS

The photoelectron yield \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa ^s_\gamma (E_\nu )$$\end{document} of atomic species s, measured at exit angle range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document} and photon energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_\nu$$\end{document}, is defined as the corresponding PE peak integral after background subtraction. To determine the PE yield of Ba and Ti atoms in the BTO thin films, Ba 4d and Ti 2p PE spectra were measured over the three exit angle ranges (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _2$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _3$$\end{document}), and the results are reported in Fig. 4. The Ti spectra in Fig. 4a show the 2p doublet at 458.8 and 464.5 eV. Spectra measured at different exit angles show the same spectral shape. This indicates that Ti atoms at the BTO surface and below experience the same chemical environment for the formation of the nominal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {Ti}^{4+}$$\end{document} state. While this is expected for Ti atoms below the surface, it holds true also for surface Ti atoms bound to oxygen-containing adsorbates14,63,64. In addition, the absence of a peak at 1.7 eV below the main Ti 2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {p}_{3/2}$$\end{document} peak provides evidence for the absence of oxygen vacancies leading to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {Ti}^{3+}$$\end{document} near the BTO surface14,63. In contrast, Ba 4d spectra, displayed in Fig. 4b, show at least two kinds of atomic species. The spin-orbit split levels Ba 4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {d}_{5/2}$$\end{document} and Ba 4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {d}_{3/2}$$\end{document} at 88.8 eV and at 91.4 eV originate from the Ba atoms below the surface and are hence referred to as the bulk component (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {Ba_{bulk}}$$\end{document}). On the other hand, the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {Ba_{surf}}$$\end{document} peaks exhibit a binding energy shift \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta \textrm{BE}=+$$\end{document} 1.2 eV and twice the FWHM compared to the bulk component. The enhancement of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {Ba_{surf}}$$\end{document} in the most surface sensitive spectrum (Ba(\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _1$$\end{document})) clearly indicates its correspondence to Ba atoms at the BTO surface, as observed in previous studies65,66. The larger FWHM is consistent with a continuity of chemical environments surrounding Ba atoms at the surface66, which could be related to different species such as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbox {OH}^-$$\end{document}, at different adsorption sites11,12, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {O_2}^-$$\end{document} species65, as suggested by the correlation with oxygen components (see Supplementary Note 4). As a result, the PE yield with the largest contribution from the surface unit cell (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _1$$\end{document}) is given by the sum of the two components (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {Ba_{surf}} + \mathrm {Ba_{bulk}}$$\end{document}) in order to include both Ba atoms at the surface and just below it, while the Ba PE yield of the deeper unit cells (at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _2$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _3$$\end{document}) is given only by the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm {Ba_{bulk}}$$\end{document} component. On the other hand, the Ti PE yield is given by the total area of the Ti doublet for all exit angle ranges. As shown above, the possibility provided by XPS to distinguish between atoms in different chemical environments enables the XSW technique to selectively determine the positions of different chemical species of the same element.
